The other day I nearly dropped a whole pitcher of sweet tea I was bringing out to Tom because I saw one land on the railing. I just get this overwhelming, blinding feeling that I have to get inside, right away, lock the door, and get as far away from it as possible. It’s like my brain just screams *run*, and I can't think about anything else until there's a solid piece of wood or glass between me and that wasp. It’s just awful, feeling that out of control so fast.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this kind of intense fear or panic in situations that don't involve wasps?\n\nPatient: No, no, not at all. It's really just the wasps. I mean, I'm a perfectly sensible person otherwise, doctor. I've always been the one in the family who keeps a level head in a crisis. When Tom's mother fell and broke her hip two years ago, I was the one who rode in the ambulance, talked to the surgeons, handled all the insurance paperwork... I didn't panic once. And like I said earlier, other bugs don't bother me like this. Spiders? I just scoop them up in a cup and put them outside. Mice? I set a trap and don't think twice about it. Even snakes, if I see a garter snake in the grass, I just let it be. \n\nBut there's just something about wasps... the way they look, the way they move. They just seem so aggressive, don't they? Like they're just looking for a reason to come after you. I don't get that feeling of pure terror anywhere else in my life. Not driving in bad weather, not flying on airplanes, not giving a speech at the church ladies' auxiliary... none of that. It is entirely, one hundred percent, just those awful, awful insects. If they didn't exist, I'd be right as rain.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of wasps affected your relationships with friends or family members beyond just your husband?\n\nPatient: Well, yes... yes, it really has, I'm afraid. It's not just Tom who has to bear the brunt of it, though heaven knows he does more than his fair share, taking over all the porch sweeping and the garden maintenance that I used to do myself. I mean, I used to be out there every evening, deadheading the geraniums and sweeping the steps, but now he has to do all of it. \n\nBut as for other people... well, it’s really put a damper on my social life, to be completely honest. I mentioned the church gardening group earlier—I had to step back from that entirely. And that wasn't just about the flowers, doctor. That was my main social circle. We’d spend hours out there, pulling weeds and chatting about our grandchildren, sharing recipes... and then we'd all go inside for coffee and cake afterward. It was wonderful. And I just can't do it now. I tried, I really did, but I’d just be standing there, rigid with fear, not hearing a word anyone was saying because I was too busy watching the sky. They stopped asking me to come after a while. They were polite about it, of course, saying they knew I was \"taking a break,\" but I know they think I'm being ridiculous.\n\nAnd it’s not just the church group. I used to love hosting garden days at my house. Every spring and summer, I’d have the ladies over for iced tea and finger sandwiches out on the patio. We’d sit under the umbrella, admire the hydrangeas—I have these beautiful blue hydrangeas that took me years to get just right with the soil acidity—and just visit for hours. But I haven't hosted a garden day in two years. I just can't bear the thought of sitting out there, trying to make polite conversation while secretly terrified that a wasp is going to fly out from under the patio table or drop down from the umbrella. I tried moving it inside, but it’s just not the same, sitting in the living room when the weather is so lovely. So, I just stopped doing it altogether. It's terribly isolating, it really is. I feel like I'm losing touch with people, all because of these horrible bugs.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of anything else besides wasps?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing else. Like I said before, I really am a very sensible person in every other respect. It's funny, actually, because my sister Brenda—she lives over in Dayton—she's terrified of just about everything. She won't fly, she won't drive on the interstate, she won't even ride in an elevator if she can help it. She says they make her feel trapped. We went to a department store once, years ago, and she made us walk up four flights of stairs just to look at the winter coats because she refused to get in the elevator. But me? Oh, I don't mind any of that. I've flown to Florida a dozen times to see my brother, and the turbulence doesn't bother me a bit. I just read my book and have a ginger ale. \n\nAnd like I was saying about the other bugs and animals... really, nothing else bothers me. I mean, nobody *likes* finding a spider in the bathtub, do they? But I just get a tissue and take care of it. I don't give it a second thought. And heights? Heights don't bother me either. When Tom and I went to the Grand Canyon for our twenty-fifth anniversary, I walked right up to the edge—well, to the railing, anyway—and looked right down. It was breathtaking, really. Tom was the one who had to step back, he said it made his stomach do flip-flops. But I felt perfectly fine. \n\nIt really is just the wasps. If you could just wave a magic wand and make every wasp in the world disappear, I wouldn't have a single fear in the world. I'd be out there in my garden right now, pruning the hydrangeas and sweeping the porch, without a care in the world. It’s just so frustrating that this one, specific, tiny little insect can have such a huge hold over my life, when I’m perfectly fine with everything else. I just don't understand it, doctor, I really don't. Why just wasps? Why not bees, even? I mean, a bumblebee will fly right past me and I just think, 'Oh, there goes a bee.' It’s all the little everyday things, too. \n\nTake groceries, for example. I just stand inside the screen door, watching him. And bless his heart, he doesn't complain, but I know it’s ridiculous. Last week, he had to carry in three twelve-packs of soda and all the heavy canned goods while I just stood there because I thought I saw something yellow near the porch light. \n\nAnd then there's the laundry. I used to love hanging the sheets out on the line in the backyard. There is absolutely nothing like the smell of sheets dried in the summer sun, is there? You just can't get that from a dryer sheet, no matter what the commercials say. But I haven't used my clothesline since last June. I was out there pinning up a pillowcase, and one of those awful things landed right on the clothespin bag. I dropped the whole basket of wet laundry in the grass and ran inside. Now, I just use the dryer for everything, even in the dead of summer, which just runs up the electric bill and heats up the whole house. \n\nEven just getting the mail has become an ordeal. Our mailbox is down at the end of the driveway, right next to this big, old crepe myrtle bush. It’s beautiful when it blooms, but it attracts every insect in the neighborhood. I won't go near it from May until October. I make Tom get the mail when he comes home from work, or if I absolutely have to check it for something important, I’ll drive the car down the driveway, roll the window down just an inch, grab the mail, and roll it right back up before I back up to the house. I mean, can you imagine? Driving down a fifty-foot driveway just to get the mail? But I just can't bring myself to walk past that bush. It’s exhausting, doctor, it really is, having to think ten steps ahead just to do the simplest chores.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other daily activities besides the ones you've mentioned that you avoid or change because of your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose there might be a few other little things here and there, but honestly, doctor, it all really just comes back to why I finally picked up the phone to make this appointment in the first place. I’m here because I’ve gotten to where I plan my whole entire summer around avoiding wasps. Not even seeing a wasp, mind you, just hearing that awful noise and completely freezing up and making Tom do all the heavy lifting. It’s just become entirely too much. I sit in the house and think, 'What am I doing?' I'm a grown woman, fifty-seven years old, and I'm letting an insect run my household. \n\nSo, whether it's the mail or the laundry or the groceries or the church, it's all just part of this giant list of things I can't do anymore. I'm just so tired of the avoiding. It's like a full-time job, trying to map out my days just to make sure I never cross paths with one of them, and I just can't keep living like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a sudden, intense fear or anxiety in response to something other than wasps, even if it was just one time?\n\nPatient: No, no, I really haven't. Not even once. I know people talk about having panic attacks or getting overwhelmed out of the blue, but I've just never been that kind of person. Like I was saying earlier about my sister Brenda, she gets worked up over the slightest little thing, but my nerves are usually steady as a rock. \n\nWhy, just last winter, Tom had a terrible scare with his chest—it turned out to be just awful indigestion from a spicy chili I made, thank goodness, but at the time we were absolutely sure it was his heart. He was clutching his chest, sweating, turning pale, and you know what? I didn't panic one bit. I didn't freeze up. I stayed perfectly calm, called the ambulance, got him an aspirin to chew on just in case, and even managed to pack a little overnight bag with his toothbrush and slippers for the hospital, all without my hands shaking once. The paramedics even complimented me on it when they arrived. They said, 'Ma'am, you are remarkably calm.' \n\nAnd it was the same when the kids were little, too. If someone fell off their bike and needed stitches or broke an arm, I was always the one holding the fort down. So, no, I don't get these sudden fits of terror about anything else in the world. It really is just... well, it's just the wasps. Honestly, if a wasp had flown into the emergency room while we were waiting for Tom's test results, *then* I probably would have lost my mind right then and there! But regular emergencies? Or other random things? No, nothing like that at all. I'm as steady as they come until I hear that buzzing.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any other activities or places because of your fear of wasps, beyond what you've already mentioned?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think... I mean, I've already told you about the garden club, and the groceries, and the laundry, and the mail... honestly, doctor, I think that really covers the bulk of it. It's like my whole world just shrinks down to the inside of my house with the windows shut tight and the air conditioning running. So, no, I don't think there are any other big activities, but the ones I've given up have just left such a huge hole in my day-to-day life, you know? It's just... it's just so exhausting living like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a similar intense fear or anxiety response to bees or other flying insects that are similar to wasps?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask that, because people always lump them together, don't they? Bees and wasps. But for me, it's completely different. I mean, if I see a bumblebee—you know, the big fuzzy ones that just kind of bumble around the clover in the yard—I don't mind them at all. I actually think they're sort of cute, in a way. They're just doing their job, pollinating the flowers, and they're so slow and clumsy. I can watch a bumblebee on a sunflower and not feel a single flutter in my chest. \n\nEven honeybees, they don't really bother me. I mean, I wouldn't go sticking my hand in a hive, obviously, I'm not crazy! But if one flies past me while I'm walking to the mailbox—well, on the days I *can* walk to the mailbox, anyway—I might just wave my hand a little to shoo it away, but I don't get that cold sweat. I don't feel like my heart is going to hammer right out of my ribs. \n\nIt really is just the wasps. The way they dart around, so fast and unpredictable. They don't just want the flowers, they want your food, your sweet tea, they just want to be in your space. A bee will leave you alone if you leave it alone, but a wasp? A wasp feels like it's out to get you. So, no, I don't have that same reaction to bees at all. It's very specific to those awful, mean-looking wasps. I used to be so involved with that. I don't get that terrible rushing feeling in my ears, or the tight chest, or the panic where I feel like I just have to run away and hide. Because, you see, a picture can't fly at me. And he shoved it right in my lap, saying, \"Look, Grandma, look how they build their houses!\" And I gasped, I really did, it startled me. I had to push the book away and tell him, \"Oh, honey, Grandma doesn't like those bugs, let's look at the butterflies instead.\" \n\nBut I didn't have a panic attack. I didn't have to leave the room or take my anxiety medicine. It was just a strong distaste, a sort of... intense revulsion, I guess you could call it. A picture is just a picture, thank goodness. If pictures did it to me too, I don't think I'd ever be able to open a magazine or turn on the television again! He never complains, bless his heart, he really doesn't. He just takes the watering can and goes out there. But I used to take such pride in keeping the outside of our house looking welcoming. I loved being the one to do it. Now I just have to watch him do my chores through the glass, hoping he doesn't stir up a nest while he's out there. \n\nSo, I suppose I don't have a whole list of new things to give you, it's just... it's the sheer weight of the things I've already lost. It has completely changed how I live my day-to-day life, from the moment I wake up until the sun finally goes down and it's safe to step outside again.\n\nDoctor: Are you able to go to work or perform your daily tasks without being anxious about encountering wasps?\n\nPatient: I know I must sound like a broken record today, doctor, I really do, but it all just ties right back into what I was telling you a moment ago. It was such a big part of how I stayed connected with people, you know? How I kept up my friendships. But now? I can't even fathom sitting out there with a glass of sweet tea. It's just asking for trouble. So I had to stop hosting those entirely. I feel terrible about it, I really do, but I just can't risk it. \n\nAnd even just around the house, my poor husband, Tom, he's had to take over so much. I used to love sweeping off the front porch in the mornings, watering the hanging baskets, sweeping away the cobwebs... just keeping things looking nice and tidy. But now, Tom has to do all of it. If the porch needs sweeping, he does it. If the plants need watering, he does it. I won't even step out there to shake out a rug if it's between May and October. It makes me feel so useless, honestly, having to rely on him for things I used to handle all by myself without a second thought. But the minute I open that front door, my eyes are just darting everywhere looking for them, and I just... I just have to go back inside. So, yes, there are quite a few things I just can't do at all anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a fear response to a picture or a video of a wasp, rather than an actual wasp?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask that, because people are always sending things on Facebook, aren't they? Little videos, or articles about garden pests, things like that. And I have to say... no, not really. I mean, if I'm scrolling through my feed and someone has posted a picture of a wasp—maybe they're asking how to get rid of a nest, or they've taken one of those extreme close-up photos, macro photography I think they call it, where you can see every horrible little hair and those awful segmented eyes... well, I certainly don't *like* looking at it. I'll scroll past it as fast as my finger can move, let me tell you. It gives me the shivers, just a little shudder down my spine, because they are just such ugly, mean-looking things. \n\nBut it's not the same fear. Not at all. I don't get that terrible rushing feeling in my ears, or the tight chest, or the panic where I feel like I just have to run away and hide. Because, you see, a picture can't fly at me. A video on my iPad isn't going to suddenly dart out of the screen and sting me on the neck. My brain knows it's flat. It's behind glass. It's trapped in the computer. \n\nI remember once, my grandson, little Timmy—he's eight now, but he was maybe six at the time—he brought over this library book about insects. He loves bugs, heaven help me, he just thinks they're fascinating. And he came running up to me on the couch, just so excited, holding this book open to a two-page spread of a yellow jacket nest. And he shoved it right in my lap, saying, \"Look, Grandma, look how they build their houses!\" And I gasped, I really did, it startled me. I had to push the book away and tell him, \"Oh, honey, Grandma doesn't like those bugs, let's look at the butterflies instead.\" \n\nBut I didn't have a panic attack. I didn't have to leave the room or take my anxiety medicine. It was just a strong distaste, a sort of... intense revulsion, I guess you could call it. But the real terror, the fear that absolutely paralyzes me and ruins my whole summer... that only happens when it's real. When I can hear that awful buzzing sound, and I know it's in the air with me, and I don't know where it's going to go next. That's what gets me. The unpredictability of the live thing. A picture is just a picture, thank goodness. If pictures did it to me too, I don't think I'd ever be able to open a magazine or turn on the television again! So, no, the pictures and videos, they gross me out, but they don't give me that true, awful fear response.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other ways your fear of wasps is affecting your daily life or routine that we haven't discussed yet?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, doctor, I feel like I might be repeating myself a bit here, because the things I mentioned earlier really are the biggest pieces of it all. I know you asked if there were other ways, but those things I brought up... they just cast such a long shadow over my whole life that it's hard to even think of anything else. \n\nLike I was saying about having to step back from the church gardening group, and stopping my own garden days... I know I already told you about that, but I don't think I can overstate how much it hurts. It's not just about missing out on the flowers or the sunshine. That was a huge part of how I stayed connected with people. When you get to be my age, and your kids are grown and busy with their own lives, you really rely on those little weekly get-togethers to keep from feeling isolated. Not being able to invite the ladies over for iced tea or go down to the sanctuary to work in the dirt together... it just makes my world feel so incredibly small. I sit inside in the air conditioning, looking out the window at my patio furniture, and it just sits there empty all summer. I miss the chatter, I miss the fellowship. It’s terribly lonely, to be completely honest with you. \n\nAnd then, of course, there's the situation at home with Tom. Like I was telling you, my husband has had to take over all those porch and garden tasks that I used to handle all by myself. And it might sound silly to dwell on it, but it really does weigh on me every single day. He has his own things to do, and the last thing he needs is to have to water my hanging baskets or sweep the front steps because his wife is too terrified to open the screen door. He never complains, bless his heart, he really doesn't. He just takes the watering can and goes out there. But I used to take such pride in keeping the outside of our house looking welcoming. I loved being the one to do it. Now I just have to watch him do my chores through the glass, hoping he doesn't stir up a nest while he's out there. \n\nSo, I suppose I don't have a whole list of new things to give you, it's just... it's the sheer weight of the things I've already lost. It has completely changed how I live my day-to-day life, from the moment I wake up until the sun finally goes down and it's safe to step outside again.\n\nDoctor: Are you able to go to work or perform your daily tasks without being anxious about encountering wasps?\n\nPatient: I know I must sound like a broken record today, doctor, I really do, but it all just ties right back into what I was telling you a moment ago. Since I don't work outside the home—I haven't for a long time now—my daily tasks, my real \"work,\" if you will, is my home, my yard, and my community activities. And I just can't do them without being absolutely eaten up by anxiety. \n\nI mean, like I said, I've had to completely step back from the church gardening group. I know I've brought it up already, but it's just such a huge loss for me. And I stopped hosting my own garden days at the house, too. Those things used to be a really big part of how I stayed connected with people! It was my social life, my way of keeping up with my friends, and now it's just gone because I can't even step out onto the patio without being terrified one of those awful things is going to swoop down on me. I just sit inside and worry about it. \n\nAnd as for my daily chores around the house, well, I mentioned my husband, Tom. He has had to take over all the porch and garden tasks that I used to handle myself. I used to be out there every morning, sweeping, watering the plants, making sure the entryway looked nice and inviting for anyone who might stop by. Now? I won't even go near the front door without my heart just pounding in my chest. I have to peek through the blinds before I even think about opening the door just a crack to grab the mail. So Tom does it all. He waters the hanging baskets, he sweeps the steps, he checks for nests. I just can't do any of my normal daily routine without this constant, looming dread, because my whole life used to revolve around being out there in the yard, and now I'm just stuck inside looking through the glass.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, my goodness, doctor, I feel like I'm just talking in circles today! I really do apologize, but yes, it’s exactly what I was just telling you about. My hobbies *were* my garden. That was my absolute passion in life, ever since Tom and I first bought the house. 

So when you ask about interests I've had to give up... well, it's the church gardening group, just like I said. That wasn't just a bit of volunteering for me, it was my main hobby! We used to plan out the flower beds for Easter, coordinate the seasonal colors, decide which mulch to use... and I just had to step back from all of it. I can't be out there in the thick of the bushes where those things like to build their nests. 

And my own garden days here at the house, too. I used to spend hours just reading up on which perennials would do best in the shade, and I'd host the ladies to show off the blooms, maybe have some cucumber sandwiches on the patio. It was my creative outlet, you know? It was how I stayed connected with people, how I spent all my free time. And now I've had to stop hosting those completely. 

Even just the little hobby-like things, like tending to my special hanging baskets on the front porch... I used to love doing that, pinching off the dead blooms and making sure they had the right fertilizer. But now, as I mentioned before, my husband has had to take over all those porch and garden tasks that I used to handle myself. It just breaks my heart because my green thumb was my whole identity, my favorite pastime, and now I'm just too frightened to even step out there to enjoy a single blossom. So yes, my main interest in life is just... well, it's gone.

    "content": "Candidate Diseases: \n[\"F40.228\", \"F41.0\"]\n\nInterview Transcript: \nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I suppose I’m here because things have just gotten... well, they've gotten completely out of hand. It’s embarrassing to admit at my age, but I’ve gotten to the point where I practically plan my entire summer around avoiding wasps. And I mean my *whole* summer. It used to be just a little nervousness, you know, swatting them away at a picnic like anyone else would. But now? Now it’s taking over. \n\nJust last month, I had to completely give up my volunteer gardening jobs down at the church. I’ve done the flower beds there for, oh, it must be fifteen years now. The petunias were my pride and joy. But I just couldn't do it anymore. The minute I stepped outside with my trowel, I’d be scanning the eaves, the bushes, every little crack in the brickwork just waiting for one of those awful things to fly out. And it’s not just at church. Even at my own house, I can't function normally. If I'm outside and I hear even the faintest buzzing sound—even if it turns out to be a bumblebee or a horsefly, which I don't mind nearly as much—I just freeze. I make my husband, Tom, go out and unload the groceries by the back porch if I think there's a wasp out there. Poor Tom, he's been so patient, but I can tell it's wearing on him. He works hard, and then he comes home and has to act as my... my bodyguard against insects. It's ridiculous, I know it is, but the panic just takes over and I can't make myself walk past where I think they might be. I just can't endure it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have episodes where you feel an intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes when you encounter or even think about wasps?\n\nPatient: Oh, yes, absolutely. It’s not just a slow, creeping worry, doctor, it’s... it's like a lightning bolt. The very second—and I mean the *very second*—I hear that sharp, angry buzzing sound, especially by the back porch where they like to build those little paper nests up in the corners of the ceiling, or if I see one of them just hovering near the flowers, it hits me all at once. \n\nMy heart just jumps right up into my throat. It starts pounding so hard I can hear it in my ears, like a drum, just *thump, thump, thump*. And my hands... oh, my hands start shaking so badly I can barely hold onto whatever I’m carrying. The other day I nearly dropped a whole pitcher of sweet tea I was bringing out to Tom because I saw one land on the railing. I just get this overwhelming, blinding feeling that I have to get inside, right away, lock the door, and get as far away from it as possible. It’s like my brain just screams *run*, and I can't think about anything else until there's a solid piece of wood or glass between me and that wasp. It’s just awful, feeling that out of control so fast.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this kind of intense fear or panic in situations that don't involve wasps?\n\nPatient: No, no, not at all. It's really just the wasps. I mean, I'm a perfectly sensible person otherwise, doctor. I've always been the one in the family who keeps a level head in a crisis. When Tom's mother fell and broke her hip two years ago, I was the one who rode in the ambulance, talked to the surgeons, handled all the insurance paperwork... I didn't panic once. And like I said earlier, other bugs don't bother me like this. Spiders? I just scoop them up in a cup and put them outside. Mice? I set a trap and don't think twice about it. Even snakes, if I see a garter snake in the grass, I just let it be. \n\nBut there's just something about wasps... the way they look, the way they move. They just seem so aggressive, don't they? Like they're just looking for a reason to come after you. I don't get that feeling of pure terror anywhere else in my life. Not driving in bad weather, not flying on airplanes, not giving a speech at the church ladies' auxiliary... none of that. It is entirely, one hundred percent, just those awful, awful insects. If they didn't exist, I'd be right as rain.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of wasps affected your relationships with friends or family members beyond just your husband?\n\nPatient: Well, yes... yes, it really has, I'm afraid. It's not just Tom who has to bear the brunt of it, though heaven knows he does more than his fair share, taking over all the porch sweeping and the garden maintenance that I used to do myself. I mean, I used to be out there every evening, deadheading the geraniums and sweeping the steps, but now he has to do all of it. \n\nBut as for other people... well, it’s really put a damper on my social life, to be completely honest. I mentioned the church gardening group earlier—I had to step back from that entirely. And that wasn't just about the flowers, doctor. That was my main social circle. We’d spend hours out there, pulling weeds and chatting about our grandchildren, sharing recipes... and then we'd all go inside for coffee and cake afterward. It was wonderful. And I just can't do it now. I tried, I really did, but I’d just be standing there, rigid with fear, not hearing a word anyone was saying because I was too busy watching the sky. They stopped asking me to come after a while. They were polite about it, of course, saying they knew I was \"taking a break,\" but I know they think I'm being ridiculous.\n\nAnd it’s not just the church group. I used to love hosting garden days at my house. Every spring and summer, I’d have the ladies over for iced tea and finger sandwiches out on the patio. We’d sit under the umbrella, admire the hydrangeas—I have these beautiful blue hydrangeas that took me years to get just right with the soil acidity—and just visit for hours. But I haven't hosted a garden day in two years. I just can't bear the thought of sitting out there, trying to make polite conversation while secretly terrified that a wasp is going to fly out from under the patio table or drop down from the umbrella. I tried moving it inside, but it’s just not the same, sitting in the living room when the weather is so lovely. So, I just stopped doing it altogether. It's terribly isolating, it really is. I feel like I'm losing touch with people, all because of these horrible bugs.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of anything else besides wasps?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing else. Like I said before, I really am a very sensible person in every other respect. It's funny, actually, because my sister Brenda—she lives over in Dayton—she's terrified of just about everything. She won't fly, she won't drive on the interstate, she won't even ride in an elevator if she can help it. She says they make her feel trapped. We went to a department store once, years ago, and she made us walk up four flights of stairs just to look at the winter coats because she refused to get in the elevator. But me? Oh, I don't mind any of that. I've flown to Florida a dozen times to see my brother, and the turbulence doesn't bother me a bit. I just read my book and have a ginger ale. \n\nAnd like I was saying about the other bugs and animals... really, nothing else bothers me. I mean, nobody *likes* finding a spider in the bathtub, do they? But I just get a tissue and take care of it. I don't give it a second thought. And heights? Heights don't bother me either. When Tom and I went to the Grand Canyon for our twenty-fifth anniversary, I walked right up to the edge—well, to the railing, anyway—and looked right down. It was breathtaking, really. Tom was the one who had to step back, he said it made his stomach do flip-flops. But I felt perfectly fine. \n\nIt really is just the wasps. If you could just wave a magic wand and make every wasp in the world disappear, I wouldn't have a single fear in the world. I'd be out there in my garden right now, pruning the hydrangeas and sweeping the porch, without a care in the world. It’s just so frustrating that this one, specific, tiny little insect can have such a huge hold over my life, when I’m perfectly fine with everything else. I just don't understand it, doctor, I really don't. Why just wasps? Why not bees, even? I mean, a bumblebee will fly right past me and I just think, 'Oh, there goes a bee.' But a wasp... it's just a whole different story.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other activities or aspects of your daily routine that you've had to change or avoid because of your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually, quite a few things. It’s gotten to the point where I feel like I plan my entire summer around avoiding them. I mean, it’s not just the gardening group at church, though that was a big loss, as I mentioned. It’s all the little everyday things, too. \n\nTake groceries, for example. We always park in the driveway, and usually, I’d just grab the bags and carry them right up the back steps to the kitchen door. But now, if I hear even the slightest buzzing sound—even if I can't see anything, just that *sound*—I freeze. I’ll make Tom come out and unload the entire car by himself. I just stand inside the screen door, watching him. And bless his heart, he doesn't complain, but I know it’s ridiculous. Last week, he had to carry in three twelve-packs of soda and all the heavy canned goods while I just stood there because I thought I saw something yellow near the porch light. \n\nAnd then there's the laundry. I used to love hanging the sheets out on the line in the backyard. There is absolutely nothing like the smell of sheets dried in the summer sun, is there? You just can't get that from a dryer sheet, no matter what the commercials say. But I haven't used my clothesline since last June. I was out there pinning up a pillowcase, and one of those awful things landed right on the clothespin bag. I dropped the whole basket of wet laundry in the grass and ran inside. Now, I just use the dryer for everything, even in the dead of summer, which just runs up the electric bill and heats up the whole house. \n\nEven just getting the mail has become an ordeal. Our mailbox is down at the end of the driveway, right next to this big, old crepe myrtle bush. It’s beautiful when it blooms, but it attracts every insect in the neighborhood. I won't go near it from May until October. I make Tom get the mail when he comes home from work, or if I absolutely have to check it for something important, I’ll drive the car down the driveway, roll the window down just an inch, grab the mail, and roll it right back up before I back up to the house. I mean, can you imagine? Driving down a fifty-foot driveway just to get the mail? But I just can't bring myself to walk past that bush. It’s exhausting, doctor, it really is, having to think ten steps ahead just to do the simplest chores.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other daily activities besides the ones you've mentioned that you avoid or change because of your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose there might be a few other little things here and there, but honestly, doctor, it all really just comes back to why I finally picked up the phone to make this appointment in the first place. I’m here because I’ve gotten to where I plan my whole entire summer around avoiding wasps. It just consumes everything, it really does. \n\nLike I was just saying, giving up my garden jobs at the church... that was a huge wake-up call for me. That wasn't just a chore I was skipping out on, that was my community, my friends! And then the groceries... making my poor husband unload things by the back porch if I even just hear a buzzing sound. Not even seeing a wasp, mind you, just hearing that awful noise and completely freezing up and making Tom do all the heavy lifting. It’s just become entirely too much. I sit in the house and think, 'What am I doing?' I'm a grown woman, fifty-seven years old, and I'm letting an insect run my household. \n\nSo, whether it's the mail or the laundry or the groceries or the church, it's all just part of this giant list of things I can't do anymore. I'm just so tired of the avoiding. It's like a full-time job, trying to map out my days just to make sure I never cross paths with one of them, and I just can't keep living like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a sudden, intense fear or anxiety in response to something other than wasps, even if it was just one time?\n\nPatient: No, no, I really haven't. Not even once. I know people talk about having panic attacks or getting overwhelmed out of the blue, but I've just never been that kind of person. Like I was saying earlier about my sister Brenda, she gets worked up over the slightest little thing, but my nerves are usually steady as a rock. \n\nWhy, just last winter, Tom had a terrible scare with his chest—it turned out to be just awful indigestion from a spicy chili I made, thank goodness, but at the time we were absolutely sure it was his heart. He was clutching his chest, sweating, turning pale, and you know what? I didn't panic one bit. I didn't freeze up. I stayed perfectly calm, called the ambulance, got him an aspirin to chew on just in case, and even managed to pack a little overnight bag with his toothbrush and slippers for the hospital, all without my hands shaking once. The paramedics even complimented me on it when they arrived. They said, 'Ma'am, you are remarkably calm.' \n\nAnd it was the same when the kids were little, too. If someone fell off their bike and needed stitches or broke an arm, I was always the one holding the fort down. So, no, I don't get these sudden fits of terror about anything else in the world. It really is just... well, it's just the wasps. Honestly, if a wasp had flown into the emergency room while we were waiting for Tom's test results, *then* I probably would have lost my mind right then and there! But regular emergencies? Or other random things? No, nothing like that at all. I'm as steady as they come until I hear that buzzing.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any other activities or places because of your fear of wasps, beyond what you've already mentioned?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think... I mean, I've already told you about the garden club, and the groceries, and the laundry, and the mail... honestly, doctor, I think that really covers the bulk of it. I mean, I'm sure if I sat down with a pen and paper and really thought about every single minute of every single day from May to October, I might come up with some little thing, like maybe not sitting on the patio furniture or avoiding the farmer's market downtown—they always have those open trash cans, you know, and those things just swarm around the sticky fruit rinds, it's a nightmare. \n\nBut no, really, I think I've pretty much laid it all out for you. It's just that those things I mentioned, they take up so much of my life. It's not just avoiding one place, it's avoiding my *own yard*. It's avoiding my own driveway. It's like my whole world just shrinks down to the inside of my house with the windows shut tight and the air conditioning running. So, no, I don't think there are any other big activities, but the ones I've given up have just left such a huge hole in my day-to-day life, you know? It's just... it's just so exhausting living like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a similar intense fear or anxiety response to bees or other flying insects that are similar to wasps?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask that, because people always lump them together, don't they? Bees and wasps. But for me, it's completely different. I mean, if I see a bumblebee—you know, the big fuzzy ones that just kind of bumble around the clover in the yard—I don't mind them at all. I actually think they're sort of cute, in a way. They're just doing their job, pollinating the flowers, and they're so slow and clumsy. I can watch a bumblebee on a sunflower and not feel a single flutter in my chest. \n\nEven honeybees, they don't really bother me. I mean, I wouldn't go sticking my hand in a hive, obviously, I'm not crazy! But if one flies past me while I'm walking to the mailbox—well, on the days I *can* walk to the mailbox, anyway—I might just wave my hand a little to shoo it away, but I don't get that cold sweat. I don't feel like my heart is going to hammer right out of my ribs. \n\nIt really is just the wasps. And the hornets, I suppose, yellow jackets, all those mean-looking ones with the pinched waists and the smooth bodies. There's just something so... aggressive about them. The way they dart around, so fast and unpredictable. They don't just want the flowers, they want your food, your sweet tea, they just want to be in your space. A bee will leave you alone if you leave it alone, but a wasp? A wasp feels like it's out to get you. So, no, I don't have that same reaction to bees at all. It's very specific to those awful, mean-looking wasps. Just thinking about the way they look, with those little dangling legs when they fly... oh, it just makes my skin crawl right now sitting here talking about it!\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily tasks or routines that you feel you can no longer perform at all because of your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, absolutely. I mean, I touched on this a little bit before, but it really has just upended my whole routine. The biggest thing, the thing that really breaks my heart, is the church gardening group. I used to be so involved with that. Every Tuesday and Thursday morning, a group of us ladies would get together and tend to the flowerbeds around the sanctuary, and we'd plan out the seasonal displays... it was just wonderful. We'd have coffee from a thermos and just chat and catch up on everyone's lives while we pulled weeds and deadheaded the petunias. But I just can't do it anymore. I had to step back completely. I haven't been to a single gardening morning since early last summer. \n\nAnd I used to host these lovely garden days at my own house, too! Oh, they were so much fun. I'd invite the ladies over, and I'd make these little cucumber sandwiches and pour iced tea, and we'd all sit out on the patio and admire the hydrangeas. It was such a big part of how I stayed connected with people, you know? How I kept up my friendships. But now? I can't even fathom sitting out there with a glass of sweet tea. It's just asking for trouble. So I had to stop hosting those entirely. I feel terrible about it, I really do, but I just can't risk it. \n\nAnd even just around the house, my poor husband, Tom, he's had to take over so much. I used to love sweeping off the front porch in the mornings, watering the hanging baskets, sweeping away the cobwebs... just keeping things looking nice and tidy. But now, Tom has to do all of it. If the porch needs sweeping, he does it. If the plants need watering, he does it. I won't even step out there to shake out a rug if it's between May and October. It makes me feel so useless, honestly, having to rely on him for things I used to handle all by myself without a second thought. But the minute I open that front door, my eyes are just darting everywhere looking for them, and I just... I just have to go back inside. So, yes, there are quite a few things I just can't do at all anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a fear response to a picture or a video of a wasp, rather than an actual wasp?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask that, because people are always sending things on Facebook, aren't they? Little videos, or articles about garden pests, things like that. And I have to say... no, not really. I mean, if I'm scrolling through my feed and someone has posted a picture of a wasp—maybe they're asking how to get rid of a nest, or they've taken one of those extreme close-up photos, macro photography I think they call it, where you can see every horrible little hair and those awful segmented eyes... well, I certainly don't *like* looking at it. I'll scroll past it as fast as my finger can move, let me tell you. It gives me the shivers, just a little shudder down my spine, because they are just such ugly, mean-looking things. \n\nBut it's not the same fear. Not at all. I don't get that terrible rushing feeling in my ears, or the tight chest, or the panic where I feel like I just have to run away and hide. Because, you see, a picture can't fly at me. A video on my iPad isn't going to suddenly dart out of the screen and sting me on the neck. My brain knows it's flat. It's behind glass. It's trapped in the computer. \n\nI remember once, my grandson, little Timmy—he's eight now, but he was maybe six at the time—he brought over this library book about insects. He loves bugs, heaven help me, he just thinks they're fascinating. And he came running up to me on the couch, just so excited, holding this book open to a two-page spread of a yellow jacket nest. And he shoved it right in my lap, saying, \"Look, Grandma, look how they build their houses!\" And I gasped, I really did, it startled me. I had to push the book away and tell him, \"Oh, honey, Grandma doesn't like those bugs, let's look at the butterflies instead.\" \n\nBut I didn't have a panic attack. I didn't have to leave the room or take my anxiety medicine. It was just a strong distaste, a sort of... intense revulsion, I guess you could call it. But the real terror, the fear that absolutely paralyzes me and ruins my whole summer... that only happens when it's real. When I can hear that awful buzzing sound, and I know it's in the air with me, and I don't know where it's going to go next. That's what gets me. The unpredictability of the live thing. A picture is just a picture, thank goodness. If pictures did it to me too, I don't think I'd ever be able to open a magazine or turn on the television again! So, no, the pictures and videos, they gross me out, but they don't give me that true, awful fear response.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other ways your fear of wasps is affecting your daily life or routine that we haven't discussed yet?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, doctor, I feel like I might be repeating myself a bit here, because the things I mentioned earlier really are the biggest pieces of it all. I know you asked if there were other ways, but those things I brought up... they just cast such a long shadow over my whole life that it's hard to even think of anything else. \n\nLike I was saying about having to step back from the church gardening group, and stopping my own garden days... I know I already told you about that, but I don't think I can overstate how much it hurts. It's not just about missing out on the flowers or the sunshine. That was a huge part of how I stayed connected with people. When you get to be my age, and your kids are grown and busy with their own lives, you really rely on those little weekly get-togethers to keep from feeling isolated. Not being able to invite the ladies over for iced tea or go down to the sanctuary to work in the dirt together... it just makes my world feel so incredibly small. I sit inside in the air conditioning, looking out the window at my patio furniture, and it just sits there empty all summer. I miss the chatter, I miss the fellowship. It’s terribly lonely, to be completely honest with you. \n\nAnd then, of course, there's the situation at home with Tom. Like I was telling you, my husband has had to take over all those porch and garden tasks that I used to handle all by myself. And it might sound silly to dwell on it, but it really does weigh on me every single day. He has his own things to do, and the last thing he needs is to have to water my hanging baskets or sweep the front steps because his wife is too terrified to open the screen door. He never complains, bless his heart, he really doesn't. He just takes the watering can and goes out there. But I used to take such pride in keeping the outside of our house looking welcoming. I loved being the one to do it. Now I just have to watch him do my chores through the glass, hoping he doesn't stir up a nest while he's out there. \n\nSo, I suppose I don't have a whole list of new things to give you, it's just... it's the sheer weight of the things I've already lost. It has completely changed how I live my day-to-day life, from the moment I wake up until the sun finally goes down and it's safe to step outside again.\n\nDoctor: Are you able to go to work or perform your daily tasks without being anxious about encountering wasps?\n\nPatient: I know I must sound like a broken record today, doctor, I really do, but it all just ties right back into what I was telling you a moment ago. Since I don't work outside the home—I haven't for a long time now—my daily tasks, my real \"work,\" if you will, is my home, my yard, and my community activities. And I just can't do them without being absolutely eaten up by anxiety. \n\nI mean, like I said, I've had to completely step back from the church gardening group. I know I've brought it up already, but it's just such a huge loss for me. And I stopped hosting my own garden days at the house, too. Those things used to be a really big part of how I stayed connected with people! It was my social life, my way of keeping up with my friends, and now it's just gone because I can't even step out onto the patio without being terrified one of those awful things is going to swoop down on me. I just sit inside and worry about it. \n\nAnd as for my daily chores around the house, well, I mentioned my husband, Tom. He has had to take over all the porch and garden tasks that I used to handle myself. I used to be out there every morning, sweeping, watering the plants, making sure the entryway looked nice and inviting for anyone who might stop by. Now? I won't even go near the front door without my heart just pounding in my chest. I have to peek through the blinds before I even think about opening the door just a crack to grab the mail. So Tom does it all. He waters the hanging baskets, he sweeps the steps, he checks for nests. I just can't do any of my normal daily routine without this constant, looming dread, because my whole life used to revolve around being out there in the yard, and now I'm just stuck inside looking through the glass.\n\nDoctor: Are there any hobbies or interests that you have had to give up or avoid because of your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: Oh, my goodness, doctor, I feel like I'm just talking in circles today! I really do apologize, but yes, it’s exactly what I was just telling you about. My hobbies *were* my garden. That was my absolute passion in life, ever since Tom and I first bought the house. \n\nSo when you ask about interests I've had to give up... well, it's the church gardening group, just like I said. That wasn't just a bit of volunteering for me, it was my main hobby! We used to plan out the flower beds for Easter, coordinate the seasonal colors, decide which mulch to use... and I just had to step back from all of it. I can't be out there in the thick of the bushes where those things like to build their nests. \n\nAnd my own garden days here at the house, too. I used to spend hours just reading up on which perennials would do best in the shade, and I'd host the ladies to show off the blooms, maybe have some cucumber sandwiches on the patio. It was my creative outlet, you know? It was how I stayed connected with people, how I spent all my free time. And now I've had to stop hosting those completely. \n\nEven just the little hobby-like things, like tending to my special hanging baskets on the front porch... I used to love doing that, pinching off the dead blooms and making sure they had the right fertilizer. But now, as I mentioned before, my husband has had to take over all those porch and garden tasks that I used to handle myself. It just breaks my heart because my green thumb was my whole identity, my favorite pastime, and now I'm just too frightened to even step out there to enjoy a single blossom. So yes, my main interest in life is just... well, it's gone."
